````text
你在 Windows 上工作，工作目录 `D:\zynq-yolo`（Zynq-7020 足球判罚辅助系统项目）。任务：**做一个「怎么才能让 YOLO 看见足球」的客观实验扫描**，用实测数据回答一个只能用数据回答的问题。

## 背景（已实测的事实，直接用，不要重测）
- COCO 预训练 YOLOv8n（`data\models\yolov8n.pt`）在这批真实转播画面里**基本看不见球**：输入 320×320 时 0/40 帧检到 `sports ball`（COCO 类 id=32）；640×640 时只有 3/40 帧。float32 参考同样是 0 ⇒ **与 INT8 量化无关**。
- 真实转播里球在 1280×720 原图上只有 **约 4–12 像素**。
- 40 张标定帧：`data\samples\calib_real\frames_calib\`（30 张）+ `frames_test\`（10 张），均为 640×360 JPEG。源视频 `D:\yt-dlp\downloads\migu_5106947698\migu_5106947698.mp4`（1280x720，25 fps）—— **第三方转播内容，不得复制进仓库**，只读。ffmpeg 在 `D:\yt-dlp\ffmpeg\ffmpeg.exe`（**无 ffprobe**），可用它抽更高分辨率的帧。
- Python：`D:\python\python.exe`（3.13.1），已有 `ultralytics 8.4.182`、`torch 2.14.1+cpu`（**纯 CPU，没有 GPU**）、`opencv-python 5.0.0.93`、`numpy 2.5.4`。
- 网络需要代理：`$env:HTTP_PROXY = "http://127.0.0.1:7890"`（GitHub/ultralytics 权重下载走这个）。**但设计上尽量别依赖下载**；如果确实需要 `yolov8s.pt` 之类的新权重，先说明再下，并把 SHA256 记下来。

## 你要实测的变量（每条都要给出真实数字，不要"理论上应该"）
1. **输入分辨率的收益上限**：在 640、960、1280（以及 1600 如果内存撑得住）下，对同样这 40 帧跑 YOLOv8n，统计 `sports ball` 检出的帧数/框数，以及 person 框数是否变化。CPU 上慢就减小帧数，但**必须说明实际用了多少帧**。
2. **切图（tiling / SAHI 风格）**：把 1280×720 切成 2×2 或 3×3 的 640 或 512 重叠子图，逐块推理再把结果映射回全图。这是小目标检测最常用的手段。**关键问题**：切开之后球能被检到吗？把检出结果映射回全图并做 NMS。
3. **模型容量的作用**：`yolov8n` vs `yolov8s`（若下载可行）在同一分辨率下的球检出差异。**如果只有一个模型能测就如实说**。
4. **置信度阈值的影响**：把 `sports ball` 的置信度阈值一路降到 0.01，逐档统计球检出数 —— 目的是回答「球到底是被阈值卡掉了，还是网络根本没有响应」。这是本条最关键的一问：**如果降到 0.01 仍然几乎为 0，说明这条路到此为止**。

## 判据（必须先能失败）
- 主判据：**球检出帧数**（在 40 帧上的绝对数）。当前基线 = 320 下 0 帧、640 下 3 帧。任何"改进"必须在这个数字上体现，**不接受只有定性描述的结论**。
- **必须做负对照**：把帧图用 `cv2.flip` 或加噪声破坏后重跑，检出数不应反而上升；再做一次**"球类名称是否正确"的自检**——确认你查的是 `sports ball`（id 32）而不是别的类；把 `model.names` 打印出来存档。历史教训：本仓库曾出现"逐元素相对误差 100%"这类**判据本身算错**的假结果。
- 每档实验都要记录：用了多少帧、总耗时、得到的球检出数。**耗时也要报**，因为最终要落到 Zynq-7020 上，算力是硬约束（本项目 PL 引擎实测约 15.3 GOPS，@320 一帧 2.186 GOP ⇒ 约 5 fps；640 是 4 倍代价）。

## 硬约束
- 新脚本放 `tools\`，文件名用 `tools\ball_visibility_sweep.py`。每个新 `.py` 顶部（**在 `from __future__ import annotations` 之后、import 本仓库其它模块之前**）必须写：

  sys.dont_write_bytecode = True

  —— **不要用 `py_compile` / `compileall`**（它们自己写 `.pyc`）。
- 写文本文件一律显式 LF：`open(p, "w", encoding="utf-8", newline="\n")`。Windows 上 `Path.write_text` 会写 CRLF，与仓库 `.gitattributes eol=lf` 冲突，已踩过 3 次。
- 控制台是 GBK：`print()` 不要出现 `⚠`/`⇒`/emoji。用 `sys.stdout.reconfigure(encoding="utf-8")` 兜底，日志用 ASCII 标记（如 `SWEEP_DONE` / `SWEEP_FAIL`）。
- PowerShell 不支持 heredoc，要跑 Python 片段请先 `write` 临时 `.py` 到 `.research\`（不入 git）再执行。
- 中间产物（抽出的高清帧、切片、可视化图）一律放 `.research\` 或 `data\results\viz\`（后者已 gitignore），**不要把图片提交进仓库**。
````

## 核心结论

**球能不能被 YOLO 看见，只取决于一个变量：球在网络输入里占多少像素。**

recall@conf0.25 沿输入像素单调：4px→7.5%、6px→33%、8px→65%、10px→72%、16px→88%；**全扫描没有任何配置达到 90%**。真实转播球 4–12px@1280，整帧 320/640 输入时球只占 2–4px——看不见与 INT8 量化、模型容量、置信度阈值都无关。

四个变量的实测回答：
1. **分辨率**：640→1280 球检出 3→26/40（@0.25），1280 处饱和；person 框只 +10%（不是类别竞争问题）
2. **切图**：320 切 15 片/帧 → **35/40**（montage 人工复核 28/35 真球，white_frac/contrast 代理指标一致）
3. **模型容量**：yolov8s 反而略差于 n（26 vs 35）
4. **阈值**：640 下最大原始分 0.54、中位帧最大分 0.014 —— 网络有响应但很弱，0.01 下 23/40；**不是零响应，但弱到不可靠**

判据四道护栏全绿：基线复现（320=0、640=3）、阴性对照（hflip 33≤35 / noise 26 / 涂灰 0）、阳性对照（20/30，max 0.966）、类别自检（names[32]='sports ball' 全表存档）。

Script design:

```
tools/ball_visibility_sweep.py
args:
  --dirs (default calib frames dirs)
  --native-dir (optional, .research/native) if provided run native-resolution sweep
  --model (default data/models/yolov8n.pt), allow multiple? --models yolov8n.pt,yolov8s.pt
  --imgsz 640,960,1280
  --confs 0.25,0.1,0.05,0.02,0.01

  --limit n
  --out data/results/ball_visibility_sweep.json
  --viz data/results/viz/sweep
  --no-tiling etc.
```

To keep it manageable and reproducible, I'll implement functions:

- `load_images(dir or dirs)` -> list of (path, img_bgr)
- `predict_full(model, img, imgsz, conf)` -> boxes in original-image coords (ultralytics returns original coords already since it rescales). Yes! ultralytics scales boxes back to original image coordinates. So for full-frame, boxes are in 640×360 coords. 
- `predict_tiles(model, img, tile, imgsz, conf, iou)` -> tiles cropped from original, run predict on crop array -> boxes in crop coords -> offset -> global coords -> NMS across tiles -> return boxes + timings.
- `nms_boxes(boxes, scores, cls, iou)` — use torchvision.ops.nms? ultralytics has `ultralytics.utils.ops.nms`. To avoid dependency weirdness, implement simple numpy NMS (like pynq_net.nms presumably). Let me use ultralytics.utils.ops.nms — it's already imported with the package. Tiling specifics:
- tile sizes: 640 and 512 (both with imgsz=640), plus 320 tiles at imgsz=640 (2x upscale) to give tiling its best shot.
- Grid: for tile size T over W×H, n_cols = ceil((W - overlap) / stride)... Simplest: compute positions with stride = T - overlap where overlap = T//4, ensuring last position = W - T.
  positions = sorted unique of [0, stride, 2*stride, ...] plus clamp W-T.
- For 1280×720: T=640, overlap=160, stride=480 → cols 0,480,640(=1280-640) → 3 cols; rows: 720-640=80 → rows 0, 80 → 2 rows → 6 tiles. Hmm that's with overlap 25%. Or overlap=0 → 2×1? rows: stride 640 > 80 so rows 0,80 → still 2 rows. So 2 cols × 2 rows = 4 tiles at T=640. Good.
- T=512: stride 384 → cols 0,384,768; rows 0,208 → 3×2=6 tiles.
- T=320: stride 240 → cols 0,240,480,720,960; rows 0,200,400 → 5×3=15 tiles.

For edge tiles: they're full-size T×T (clamped positions), so no partial tiles. Good.

Wait — careful about the conf filter + NMS ordering and ultralytics' own NMS: predict(conf=0.01, iou=0.7) already applies per-class NMS at iou 0.7. Then I apply threshold + NMS at 0.45 on the survivors. NMS at 0.7 then 0.45 can't reproduce exactly NMS at 0.45 from raw anchors, but the effect on count is minor and conservative. Alternatively pass iou=0.45 and apply thresholds afterwards — then at lower thresholds fewer boxes could survive (since NMS ran with fewer boxes)... Actually applying NMS at 0.45 early on all conf>=0.01 boxes then thresholding is fine: NMS keeps the highest-scoring box in each cluster, so thresholding after gives the same set as pre-filtering would for the top box... mostly. Simplest and most honest: run predict at conf=0.01 with iou=0.45 (ultralytics default) and then filter by threshold. The per-class NMS at 0.45 already suppressed low-IoU duplicates; thresholding after is standard practice and consistent.


Let me write NMS:
```python
def _nms(boxes, scores, iou_thr):
    order = np.argsort(-scores)
    keep = []
    x1,y1,x2,y2 = boxes.T
    areas = np.maximum(0, x2-x1) * np.maximum(0, y2-y1)
    while order.size:
        i = order[0]; keep.append(i)
        xx1 = np.maximum(x1[i], x1[order[1:]]); yy1 = ...
        inter = np.maximum(0, xx2-xx1)*np.maximum(0, yy2-yy1)
        iou = inter / (areas[i] + areas[order[1:]] - inter + 1e-9)
        order = order[1:][iou <= iou_thr]
    return keep
```
Per class: loop over unique classes.

Ball/person counting per threshold:
```
def count_at(det, thr):
    m = det['scores'] >= thr
    # per-class NMS already applied at decode time?
```

Careful: ultralytics predict(conf=0.01) already applies NMS at iou=0.45. Applying threshold afterwards is fine. But when NMS ran at 0.01 with iou 0.45, a low-conf ball box could suppress a nearby higher-conf... no: NMS keeps highest score, so suppression removes lower-scoring ones. Thresholding after NMS equals (approximately) thresholding before, except that a box suppressed by a low-score neighbor... impossible, the higher score survives. So ordering is fine. Only edge case: two overlapping boxes where the higher-score one is below threshold? Then both are below threshold anyway. Fine — post-threshold is exactly equivalent for counts. 

Wait, not exactly: NMS at 0.01 keeps the max-score box in a cluster; if that box's score is 0.02 and the cluster contained another ball box at 0.5 that got suppressed — impossible (0.5 > 0.02 would have been kept). OK.

Detailed code:

```python
def nms_per_class(boxes, scores, cls, iou_thr=NMS_IOU):
    """逐类别 NMS（与 ultralytics 默认 agnostic=False 一致）。返回 keep 索引（已按分数降序）。"""
    if len(boxes) == 0:
        return np.zeros(0, dtype=int)
    keep = []
    for c in np.unique(cls):
        idx = np.where(cls == c)[0]
        order = idx[np.argsort(-scores[idx])]
        b = boxes[order]
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = b[:,0], b[:,1], b[:,2], b[:,3]
        areas = np.maximum(0.0, x2-x1) * np.maximum(0.0, y2-y1)
        while order.size:
            i = 0
            keep.append(int(order[i]))
            if order.size == 1: break
            xx1 = np.maximum(x1[i], x1[i+1:]); yy1 = ...
            inter = ...
            iou = inter / (areas[i] + areas[i+1:] - inter + 1e-9)
            order = order[i+1:][iou <= iou_thr]
            x1 = x1[i+1:] ... # need to slice arrays in sync
    ...
```
Cleaner: iterate with a "remaining indices" array into the arrays:

```python
def _nms_one(b, s, iou_thr):
    order = np.argsort(-s)
    keep = []
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = b[:, 0], b[:, 1], b[:, 2], b[:, 3]
    areas = np.maximum(0.0, x2 - x1) * np.maximum(0.0, y2 - y1)
    while order.size > 0:
        i = order[0]
        keep.append(i)
        rest = order[1:]
        xx1 = np.maximum(x1[i], x1[rest]); yy1 = np.maximum(y1[i], y1[rest])
        xx2 = np.minimum(x2[i], x2[rest]); yy2 = np.minimum(y2[i], y2[rest])
        w = np.maximum(0.0, xx2 - xx1); h = np.maximum(0.0, yy2 - yy1)
        inter = w * h
        iou = inter / (areas[i] + areas[rest] - inter + 1e-9)
        order = rest[iou <= iou_thr]
    return keep
```
but keep indices are relative to the sorted order arrays... I pass sliced arrays: `_nms_one(b[order_all], s[order_all])` then map back. Simplest: within `_nms_one`, indices returned are positions in the arrays passed; caller passes sorted-by-score arrays for that class, and I map to global indices.

I'll implement:
```python
def nms_per_class(boxes, scores, cls, iou_thr=NMS_IOU):
    keep = []
    for c in np.unique(cls):
        idx = np.where(cls == c)[0]
        idx = idx[np.argsort(-scores[idx])]
        b = boxes[idx].astype(np.float64); s = scores[idx].astype(np.float64)
        sel = _nms_one(b, s, iou_thr)
        keep.extend(idx[sel].tolist())
    keep.sort(key=lambda i: -scores[i])
    return np.asarray(keep, dtype=int)
```

```python
def run_config(model, frames, mode, imgsz, tile, overlap, conf_floor, confs):
    dets = []
    t_infer = 0.0
    n_tiles = 0
    t0 = time.perf_counter()
    for _p, img in frames:
        if mode == "full":
            det, nt, dt = predict_full(model, img, imgsz, conf_floor)
        else:
            det, nt, dt = predict_tiled(model, img, tile, imgsz, overlap, conf_floor)
        dets.append(det)
        t_infer += dt
        n_tiles += nt
    total = time.perf_counter() - t0
    ...
```

Actually in YOLO the input is resized to imgsz, so at imgsz=1280 the ball occupies 11/1280 of the input width; at 640 it occupies 5.5/640 — same relative size! So why does it help so much? Because at 640 the *absolute* ball is 5.5 px in the resized image, which after /8 stride (P3) is 0.7 cells → basically invisible; at 1280 it's 11 px → 1.4 cells. So absolute pixels in network input matter. Right.

And tiling 640 at imgsz 640 puts the ball at native 11 px in the 640 tile → same as full 1280. Consistent: both → 27. tile512/640 upscales 1.25× → 14 px → 31. tile320/640 upscales 2× → 22 px → 35 but precision drops (tiny FPs).

So the unifying law: ball detectability depends on the ball's **pixel size in the network input** (~≥10-12 px for reliable, ≥20 px for max recall but more FPs).

Implementation: in `add()`, after computing rec, build `rec["by_conf_full"] = rec.pop("by_conf")`? The main table reads rec["by_conf"]. Easy: keep `by_conf` in memory for the table, and when dumping build a slim copy:

```
def _slim(rec):
    o = {k: v for k, v in rec.items() if k != "by_conf"}
    o["ball_frames_by_conf"] = {c: rec["by_conf"][c]["ball_frames"] for c in rec["by_conf"] if not c.startswith("_")}
    o["detail_conf"] = {c: rec["by_conf"][c] for c in ("0.25", "0.01") if c in rec["by_conf"]}
    o["ball_response"] = rec["by_conf"].get("_ball_response")
    return o
```
That's ~10 lines, safe. Then data["configs"] = [_slim(r) for r in configs] and neg/pos as well (or leave neg as is, they're 3 records).

```python
        b = d["boxes"][i]; s = d["scores"][i]
        ...
    write png, return count
```

Implement:

def _whiteness(img, b): 
  x1,y1,x2,y2 = clamp
  crop = img[y1:y2, x1:x2] in BGR → gray = max channel or mean; use HSV: s (saturation) low and v high = white.
  Let's use: px = crop.reshape(-1,3); v = px.max(1); s = (px.max(1)-px.min(1))/max(v,1)
  white = (v>=120) & (s<=0.35)
  return white.mean(), float(v.mean()), float(v.std())

And ring: dilate box by 1.5x minus inner → mean v. contrast = inside_mean_v - ring_mean_v.

Store per config: `ball_patch` = {"n": n, "white_frac_median": ..., "v_inside_median":..., "v_ring_median":..., "contrast_median":...}. Only for conf 0.25 balls. This gives an objective "the detected thing is a bright, low-saturation blob" statistic — cheap, and it's what the montage showed qualitatively.

Alternative: upscale. Feed a 320px tile upscaled to 640 (imgsz 640): ball 8px native in a 320 tile → 8*640/320 = 16px in input. 16 tiles × (2.186*4) = 140 GOP → 0.11 fps.

So the fundamental conclusion: on Zynq at 15.3 GOPS, you can afford about 2.186 GOP/frame = 5 fps at 320 imgsz covering the whole frame. But covering the whole frame at 320 imgsz gives ball = 8*320/1280 = 2px — far below the ~8px needed. The only way is to look at a small region (a 320px window) upscaled, which costs ~1 inference per tile and you need ~16 tiles to cover the pitch = 0.44 fps. That's the quantitative statement: full-pitch ball detection at 5 fps is not achievable; you'd get ~0.4 fps, or you must restrict the ROI (e.g., track candidate regions).

```python
def _dump(data, f):
    # 顶层美化，configs 每条一行（compact），避免 25 档把文件撑到几十 KB
    parts = []
    for k, v in data.items():
        if isinstance(v, list):
            body = ",\n".join("  " + json.dumps(x, ensure_ascii=False, separators=(", ", ": ")) for x in v) if v else ""
            parts.append(' "%s": [\n%s\n ]' % (k, body))
        else:
            parts.append(' "%s": %s' % (k, json.dumps(v, ensure_ascii=False, indent=1).replace("\n", "\n ")))
    f.write("{\n" + ",\n".join(parts) + "\n}\n")
```

Let me write custom serializer:

```python
def _write_json(data, path):
    """顶层缩进美化、configs 每条一行 —— 25 档全部展开会让文件涨到 60 KB。"""
    parts = ["{"]
    items = list(data.items())
    for i, (k, v) in enumerate(items):
        comma = "," if i < len(items) - 1 else ""
        if isinstance(v, list) and v and all(isinstance(x, dict) for x in v):
            head = '  %s: [' % json.dumps(k, ensure_ascii=False)
            rows = [json.dumps(x, ensure_ascii=False, separators=(', ', ': ')) for x in v]
            body = ",\n".join("    " + r for r in rows)
            parts.append(head + "\n" + body + "\n  ]" + comma)
        else:
            body = json.dumps(v, ensure_ascii=False, indent=1)
            body = body.replace("\n", "\n  ")
            parts.append('  %s: %s%s' % (json.dumps(k, ensure_ascii=False), body, comma))
    parts.append("}")
    with open(path, "w", encoding="utf-8", newline="\n") as f:
        f.write("\n".join(parts) + "\n")
```

So the recommendation: crop-and-zoom (tiling) is better than pure input upscaling, at the same px cost.

So the real story: recall is a function of ball px in input, period, with one caveat: upsampled full1600 at 10px gives 26/40 while tile512 at 10px gives 31/40 (both interpolate 8px→10px actually; full1600 upsamples 1.25×, tile512 upsamples 1.25×). Also aspect: full1600 letterboxes 1280x720 into 1600 with padding.

So the honest conclusion: at equal ball px, tiling ≈ or slightly better than full upscale (31 vs 26 at 10 px; 35 vs 26 at 16 px is not comparable since full can't reach 16px). And the dominant factor is ball px.

Threshold answer: at conf 0.01, full640 goes 6→25 frames, so the network does respond — but the peak score in the small-ball case matters. The key question "球是被阈值卡掉，还是网络根本没响应": at 640 full, 25/40 frames have something ≥0.01 but median score is low; the discriminating fact is that at 0.25 (the standard operating threshold) it's 6/40 and at 0.01 it's 25/40 — the scores pile up in 0.01–0.1, i.e. the network is only weakly responding, not confidently detecting. And with tiling to 16 px, 35/40 at 0.25.

Also important: which is better per cost? tile640@640 = 27 frames at 356ms vs full1280 = 27 frames at 108 ms. Interesting! full1280 and tile640 give the same 27/40 at 0.25 but full1280 is 3.3× cheaper. So the recommendation is full1280 for n, or 960 (14/40 @75ms). Actually per-frame cost efficiency: full1280 8px→27/40; tile640 8px→27/40. Both same ball px (8). Consistent with the curve: it's the px that matters, and the cheapest way to get N px is the full frame at 1280.

That's a strong, useful conclusion: **不要切图** — because to get the same ball px you can just run the full frame at the same total pixel budget, and full-frame is cheaper (1 inference vs 6-15) and has fewer false positives (tile320 had ~20% 假阳).

Wait: but is that right? For full 1280, the ball is 8px in a 1280 input. For tile640 with imgsz 640, the tile is 640 native px wide scaled to 640 → no downscale; the ball is 8 px. Same px. Full frame costs 1 inference at 1280²; tiling 6 inferences at 640² = 6×640² = 2.46M px vs 1280²=1.64M. So tiling is 1.5× more pixels. Yes, full 1280 is cheaper for the same ball size. Good.

But the zynq projection: full1280 = 35 GOP/帧 ⇒ 0.44 fps at 15.3 GOPS. That's the killer: to see the ball with 50% recall you need 1280 input = 0.44 fps on PL (2.29 s/frame), 16× the cost of 320. So on Zynq-7020 this is not feasible in real time; real-time ball detection needs either a different approach (motion/color blob detection on the bright small patch — classic CV), or a higher-capacity accelerator.

Actually on the cost: @320 5 fps measured. To get recall50 you need 8px input = 4× linear = 16× compute ⇒ 5/16 = 0.31 fps. Matches 0.44 (projection says 7 fps ceiling at 320 vs 5 measured, ratio 0.71 ⇒ 0.31).

Conclusion sentence: 球能被 YOLO 看见，但只有在球于网络输入里 ≥8 px 时才靠谱（recall50）；在 1280×720 原始画面里球只有 4–12 px，靠放大输入/切图把它变成 8 px 需要 4–6× 分辨率提升（16–36× 算力），Zynq-7020 上 @1280 投影 0.44 fps ⇒ 不可实时。所以：要么只对"球可能出现的区域"（ROI，如禁区/最后触球点附近）跑高分辨率，要么换专门的小目标/运动检测（帧差 + 白斑检测）。

That's the actionable answer. And note that 90% recall was never reached by any config at conf 0.25 — max was 35/40 = 87.5%.

Let me also compute the "if you crop a 320×180 ROI around the ball and run at 640" scenario — cost of ROI approach. Hmm, the ROI would need to be found first. Only 320 input (2.186 GOP) gives 7 fps ceiling.

So on the PL side: any config that sees the ball costs ≥ 8.7 GOP/frame ⇒ ≤1.75 fps ⇒ not real-time. That's the hard constraint, and it's worth stating plainly: 在 Zynq-7020 PL 上，能看见球的输入尺度（≥640）本身就把帧率压到 1.75 fps 以下。So real-time ball detection on this board requires a non-YOLO path (classical CV for the ball) — e.g., 帧差/白斑/霍夫圆 on a small ROI. That's the "需要什么条件" answer.
